      # company.md

      # Company profile


      ## Company Name


      Mosaic Relay


      ## Website Url


      Not provided.


      ## Summary


      Mosaic Relay is a fictional payment infrastructure company created only for
      this Counsel OS UX experiment. It has no real-company affiliation and must not
      be confused with Stripe, Checkout.com, Marqeta, Manifest Financial, Chime, or
      any other real company. Business model: Mosaic Relay sells API and hosted infrastructure
      to online businesses, marketplaces, and software platforms. It charges platform
      and usage fees for payment acceptance, risk, operations, and payout tools. It
      is not a bank. It does not offer bank accounts, hold deposits, take deposits,
      or operate as a bank. Products and services: APIs and hosted tools for card,
      ACH, and local payment acceptance; payment routing and splits; refunds and disputes;
      seller, contractor, and recipient payouts; merchant onboarding; identity verification;
      KYB and KYC; sanctions screening; fraud monitoring; transaction risk scoring;
      payout controls; reconciliation; and operations dashboards. Customers: online
      businesses, marketplaces, and software platforms, including businesses that
      onboard sellers, contractors, or recipients. Mosaic Relay supports their payment
      operations but does not take custody of customer deposits. Partners and dependencies:
      acquiring banks, payment processors, card networks, identity and fraud vendors,
      and cloud providers. These are third-party relationships, not ownership or affiliation.
      Footprint and teams: fictional US-headquartered company serving customers in
      North America, the UK, EU, and selected other markets through remote and regional
      teams. Core teams are product and engineering, payments operations, risk and
      trust, compliance and financial crime, sales and customer success, security
      and privacy, finance, and legal. Legal topics: BSA/AML and financial-crime controls;
      money transmission and payments regulation; card-network and processor rules;
      ACH and local-payment rules; consumer protection and funds-flow disclosures;
      privacy and data protection; cybersecurity and incident response; sanctions;
      KYB/KYC and identity; vendor and cloud contracts; commercial terms; IP; employment;
      and marketing compliance. Risk posture: moderate-to-high regulatory and operational
      risk because the platform supports payment acceptance, onboarding, risk decisions,
      and payouts. Mosaic Relay uses risk-based controls, clear ownership, documented
      escalation, auditability, vendor oversight, data minimization, and human review
      for material exceptions. It aims to prevent fraud, sanctions breaches, unsafe
      payouts, and misleading customer claims while keeping the product usable. Operating
      principles: be accurate about what Mosaic Relay does and does not do; separate
      recommendations from recorded decisions; state material assumptions; preserve
      an editable work product; avoid claiming licenses, registrations, approvals,
      or bank status unless confirmed; protect personal data; and favor a useful first
      pass with clear gaps over a blank result. Fictional legal owner: Jordan Lee,
      fictional General Counsel and Head of Regulatory Affairs for Mosaic Relay. Jordan
      owns legal decisions and escalation. The company, people, partners, customers,
      and footprint described here are fictional for testing.


      ## Business Model


      Sells API and hosted infrastructure to online businesses, marketplaces, and
      software platforms. Charges platform and usage fees for payment acceptance,
      risk, operations, and payout tools. Not a bank; does not hold or take deposits.


      ## Products Services


      APIs and hosted tools for card, ACH, and local payment acceptance; payment routing
      and splits; refunds and disputes; seller, contractor, and recipient payouts;
      merchant onboarding; identity verification; KYB and KYC; sanctions screening;
      fraud monitoring; transaction risk scoring; payout controls; reconciliation;
      and operations dashboards.


      ## Jurisdictions


      US-headquartered; serves customers in North America, the UK, EU, and selected
      other markets through remote and regional teams.


      ## Regulatory Context


      Subject to BSA/AML and financial-crime controls; money transmission and payments
      regulation; card-network and processor rules; ACH and local-payment rules; consumer
      protection and funds-flow disclosures; privacy and data protection; cybersecurity
      and incident response; sanctions; KYB/KYC and identity; vendor and cloud contracts;
      commercial terms; IP; employment; and marketing compliance.


      ## Data Practices


      Processes personal data in connection with identity verification, KYB/KYC, sanctions
      screening, fraud monitoring, and transaction risk scoring. Emphasizes data minimization
      and protection of personal data.


      ## Risk Posture


      Moderate-to-high regulatory and operational risk due to payment acceptance,
      onboarding, risk decisions, and payouts. Uses risk-based controls, clear ownership,
      documented escalation, auditability, vendor oversight, data minimization, and
      human review for material exceptions. Aims to prevent fraud, sanctions breaches,
      unsafe payouts, and misleading customer claims while keeping the product usable.

  - role: assistant
    content: '**No external authority retrieved**


      ## Practical answer


      **Do not treat Harbor’s 38 money transmitter licenses as transferable assets.**
      For closing purposes, assume that Mosaic cannot use them unless each relevant
      state regulator confirms a lawful path.


      An asset purchase usually leaves the license with the licensed seller. It does
      not automatically move the license to the buyer. The buyer generally needs its
      own authority before it conducts regulated money transmission. A contract that
      says Mosaic “assumes” the licenses does not bind a regulator.


      This makes the proposed four-state expansion a closing issue. It may also affect
      Harbor customers in states where both companies already hold licenses because
      the operating entity, customer agreement, custody of funds, or licensed activity
      can change.


      **No external authority retrieved.** The research call failed. The conclusions
      below are generated analysis based on the reported transaction facts. No state
      rule, regulator guidance, or approval process was verified in this research
      run.


      ## Required pre-close work


      | Work | Why | Proposed owner | Needed by | Evidence to proceed | Fallback |

      |---|---|---|---|---|---|

      | Build a state matrix for all Harbor-licensed states | Notice, approval, surrender,
      and transition rules differ by state | Regulatory counsel with licensing team
      | Before final migration design | Written state-by-state conclusion, filing
      type, deadline, and regulator response | Keep Harbor customers outside Mosaic
      until authority is confirmed |

      | Prioritize the four states where only Harbor is licensed | Mosaic appears
      to lack independent authority there | Regulatory counsel | Before any launch
      commitment | Mosaic license, written approval, or another confirmed legal basis
      | Do not offer regulated services in those states |

      | Confirm which entity will be the legal service provider after close | The
      licensed entity must match the entity conducting the regulated activity | Deal
      counsel and product counsel | Before customer notices and contract assignment
      | Funds-flow diagram, customer contract, account terms, and operating model
      | Keep Harbor as provider during a regulator-approved transition |

      | Identify required notices and approvals | An asset sale may trigger asset-transfer,
      business-change, control, name, location, key-person, or surrender filings |
      Licensing team | Before each state deadline | Filing receipts and any required
      approvals or non-objections | Delay the affected state migration |

      | Obtain an interim-operation answer for each affected state | A pending filing
      does not necessarily permit operation | Regulatory counsel | Before close |
      Written authority, regulator confirmation, or a clearly applicable rule | Disable
      transactions or restrict access in that state |

      | Plan Harbor’s license surrender or run-off | Early surrender could remove
      the authority needed for open transactions, claims, examinations, or refunds
      | Harbor compliance and deal counsel | As part of closing conditions | Regulator-approved
      surrender or run-off plan | Keep the license, bonds, and responsible staff in
      place |

      | Protect required financial assurances | Bonds, permissible investments, net
      worth, and reporting duties may continue during transition | Finance and licensing
      team | Before funds or obligations move | Updated bonds, financial statements,
      and regulator filings | Hold affected balances and obligations in the existing
      licensed structure |


      ## Notice and approval analysis


      The team should not limit its review to provisions called “license transfer.”
      The relevant rule may instead appear under:


      - transfer of substantially all assets;

      - change in control or ownership;

      - merger or corporate reorganization;

      - change in legal entity or licensed name;

      - change in business plan;

      - change in responsible individuals;

      - change in locations or agents;

      - assignment of customer obligations;

      - surrender, suspension, or termination of a license; or

      - acquisition of outstanding payment obligations.


      A pure asset sale may not meet a state’s technical definition of “change of
      control.” That does **not** mean no filing is required. The seller’s disposition
      of the business and the buyer’s start of regulated activity can create separate
      requirements.


      Some filings may be notices after signing or before closing. Others may require
      approval before the transaction, before the buyer begins business, or before
      the seller surrenders its license. The applicable timing must be verified state
      by state.


      ## Interim operation


      Mosaic should not assume it can operate while a new-license or approval request
      is pending. A filed application usually should not be treated as operating authority
      without a verified rule or written regulator position.


      A possible transition is for Harbor to remain the licensed provider for a limited
      period. This works only if it is legally and operationally real:


      - Harbor remains responsible to customers.

      - Harbor retains control required of the licensee.

      - Customer terms identify the correct provider.

      - Customer funds and outstanding obligations remain properly covered.

      - Required bonds, permissible investments, reports, records, and examinations
      continue.

      - Mosaic’s role is permitted under applicable law.

      - The arrangement does not amount to Mosaic using Harbor’s license as a rented
      license.

      - The transaction documents keep Harbor able to perform these duties.

      - Relevant regulators approve or accept the structure when required.


      The present plan does not appear to support that model. It moves users into
      Mosaic’s app with one Mosaic account, one balance, and access to Mosaic’s products
      on day one. Those facts point toward Mosaic becoming the operating provider.
      If so, Harbor’s continuing license may not solve Mosaic’s authorization gap.


      ## Transition requirements


      The licensing plan should cover more than the four new states. It should address:


      1. **Outstanding obligations:** Identify which entity owes stored balances,
      transfers, refunds, and claims at each transition point.

      2. **Customer contracts:** Confirm whether agreements can be assigned and whether
      notice or consent is required.

      3. **Funds and safeguarding:** Map the movement of customer funds and the related
      permissible-investment coverage.

      4. **Surety bonds:** Keep required bonds active until each regulator permits
      release.

      5. **Reports and examinations:** Assign responsibility for pre-close and post-close
      reports, complaints, examinations, and record production.

      6. **Books and records:** Preserve Harbor’s required records and regulator access
      after the vendor and corporate transition.

      7. **Agents and delegates:** Determine whether any registered agents, authorized
      delegates, or locations need termination or new appointments.

      8. **Surrender:** Do not surrender Harbor’s licenses until outstanding obligations
      and regulator conditions are satisfied.

      9. **Customer communications:** Ensure notices state the correct provider, effective
      date, rights, fees, and complaint contact.

      10. **Product scope:** Confirm that Mosaic’s existing licenses cover all products
      that migrated users will receive, not only Harbor’s former service.


      ## Recommended closing position


      Treat authorization for each affected state as a **launch condition**, not as
      a post-close clean-up item.


      For each state, require one of these outcomes before Mosaic serves migrated
      customers:


      1. Mosaic already has a license that covers the planned activity and product
      set.

      2. Mosaic obtains the required license or approval.

      3. The regulator confirms a lawful interim structure.

      4. The affected customers remain with Harbor under a supportable run-off model.

      5. Regulated functionality is blocked until authority exists.


      The four Harbor-only states should not be presented as part of Mosaic’s available
      footprint until one of these paths is documented. If the state review cannot
      finish before close, the most defensible fallback is a state-based migration
      hold. Mosaic can close the asset acquisition without activating regulated service
      in those states.


      This result does not materially change the saved working position. It gives
      that position a clearer operational rule: **no regulator-confirmed authority,
      no day-one regulated operation by Mosaic in the affected state.**


      [Open full research](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-bf2e7d.md)


      [Review the research-based recommendation proposal](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/recommendations.md)
      — not a recorded decision.


      [Open dossier revision](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/dossier-revisions/DOS-5571e34d40d414ae03f8.md)'

  - role: assistant
    content: '## Practical answer


      **Do not treat Harbor’s money transmitter licenses as assets that move to Mosaic
      in the asset purchase.** Use this closing rule:


      > **No confirmed authority means no regulated launch by Mosaic in that state.**


      The asset purchase can still close. However, Mosaic should not activate regulated
      services in the four Harbor-only states unless one of these conditions is met:


      1. Mosaic obtains its own license.

      2. The state approves a transfer or other license change.

      3. The regulator confirms a lawful interim structure.

      4. Harbor remains the real licensed provider under a valid transition plan.

      5. Mosaic blocks regulated functions in that state.


      The planned unified Mosaic account is important. It suggests that Mosaic, not
      Harbor, will become the service provider. If that is correct, keeping Harbor’s
      legal entity and licenses alive might not give Mosaic authority to operate.


      **No verified operative authority retrieved.** The collection found the CSBS
      Model Money Transmission Modernization Act and a New York regulator licensing
      page. However, no exact provision about an asset sale, license transfer, interim
      operation, or custody was available for review. Also, a model act is not proof
      of the law adopted in a specific state. The state-specific conclusions below
      remain conditional.


      ## Work needed before launch


      | Work | Why | Proposed owner | Needed by | Evidence to proceed | Fallback |

      |---|---|---|---|---|---|

      | Identify the four Harbor-only states | These states have the clearest authority
      gap | Licensing team | Now | Confirmed license list and covered activities |
      Block regulated functions in those states |

      | Build a matrix for every affected state | Transfer, notice, approval, and
      surrender rules can differ | State regulatory counsel | Before the closing conditions
      are final | Applicable rule, filing deadline, regulator response, and license
      status | Delay migration in the affected state |

      | Confirm the post-close service provider | The licensed entity must match the
      actual operating model | Product and deal counsel | Before customer notices
      and account design are final | Contracts, user interface, funds-flow diagram,
      and operating roles | Keep Harbor as the actual provider where permitted |

      | Review notices and approvals | An asset sale can create separate seller and
      buyer requirements | Licensing team | Before each state deadline | Filing receipts
      and required approvals or non-objections | Make approval a state launch condition
      |

      | Confirm interim operating authority | A pending application should not be
      assumed to permit operation | State regulatory counsel | Before close | Applicable
      rule or written regulator confirmation | Keep Harbor customers segregated or
      restrict transactions |

      | Review partner-bank arrangements | Bank custody does not, by itself, establish
      Mosaic’s licensing position | Payments counsel and bank relationship owner |
      Before balances move | Bank agreements, account titles, control rights, reconciliation
      design, and bank consent | Keep funds and obligations in the existing structure
      |

      | Prepare license run-off and surrender plans | Harbor may retain duties for
      balances, claims, records, and reports | Harbor compliance and deal counsel
      | Before surrender or operational shutdown | Regulator-approved plan and continued
      bonds and financial coverage | Keep Harbor licensed and operational longer |


      ## State notice and approval


      Do not search only for a rule called “license transfer.” Review each state for:


      - sale of substantially all assets;

      - change of control;

      - change in business plan;

      - change in licensed activities;

      - assignment of outstanding payment obligations;

      - change in responsible persons, locations, or agents;

      - license surrender or termination;

      - customer agreement assignment; and

      - movement of stored balances or customer funds.


      An asset purchase might fall outside a technical “change of control” rule because
      Harbor’s ownership does not change. That does not resolve the issue. Harbor
      might still need a notice or surrender approval. Mosaic might separately need
      authority before it starts the regulated activity.


      The state matrix should distinguish:


      - notice only;

      - approval before closing;

      - approval before customer migration;

      - new license required;

      - operation permitted while an application is pending;

      - regulator-approved transition available; and

      - no supported interim path.


      ## Interim operation


      Harbor could remain the licensed provider for a transition period. However,
      the structure must be real, not only contractual language.


      A supportable structure would normally need to show that:


      - Harbor remains responsible to customers.

      - Customer terms name Harbor as the provider.

      - Harbor controls the regulated activity as required.

      - Harbor’s licenses, bonds, and financial coverage remain active.

      - Customer funds and outstanding obligations remain covered.

      - Harbor can complete reports, refunds, complaints, and examinations.

      - Required records remain available.

      - Mosaic’s operational role is permitted.

      - The partner bank accepts the structure.

      - Each relevant regulator permits or accepts the structure.


      The present plan creates tension with this approach. A single Mosaic account,
      one combined balance, and full access to Mosaic products make it harder to show
      that Harbor remains the true provider.


      ## Partner-bank custody


      A partner bank account is not automatically a substitute for a money transmitter
      license. The key questions concern the complete activity and obligation flow.


      For each customer balance and transfer, document:


      1. Who receives the customer’s instruction?

      2. Who accepts the obligation to transmit or return the funds?

      3. In whose name is the bank account held?

      4. Is it a custodial or “for benefit of” account?

      5. Who can direct withdrawals?

      6. Who owns the ledger and resolves errors?

      7. Who bears loss if a transfer fails?

      8. Which entity owes the customer a refund?

      9. Which entity’s license, bond, and permissible investments cover the obligation?

      10. Does the bank agreement permit assignment, migration, or a change in the
      program manager?

      11. Is bank consent required before closing or migration?

      12. What happens to funds if Harbor, Mosaic, or the bank relationship ends?


      A bank-led model might provide a different licensing analysis if the bank is
      the actual provider and Mosaic performs only permitted services. A bank account
      that merely holds funds for Mosaic or Harbor is different. The legal documents,
      customer terms, control rights, and actual operations must all support the claimed
      model.


      Also review:


      - account segregation;

      - daily reconciliation;

      - shortfalls and reserve funding;

      - bank setoff rights;

      - liens and security interests;

      - continuity if the account is frozen;

      - treatment of pending transfers;

      - return and chargeback duties;

      - insolvency treatment;

      - customer disclosures; and

      - whether any deposit-insurance statement is accurate for the actual account
      structure.


      ## Recommended closing position


      Keep the acquisition closing separate from the regulated-service launch.


      For the four Harbor-only states, use a state launch hold unless Mosaic has documented
      authority. For overlapping states, confirm that Mosaic’s existing licenses cover
      the complete post-close product set and funds flow.


      Do not surrender Harbor’s licenses or release its bonds until the applicable
      regulators permit it and all outstanding obligations have been assigned, completed,
      or placed into an approved run-off structure.


      This investigation does not change the current recommendation. It adds one material
      point: **partner-bank custody is part of the licensing analysis, but custody
      alone does not cure an authority gap.**


      [Open full research](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/research/RES-20260911-0a5f07.md)


      [Review the research-based recommendation proposal](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/recommendations.md)
      — not a recorded decision.


      [Open dossier revision](03_Matters/harbor-2-d89ad8/dossier-revisions/DOS-4cc39b05492c9b214333.md)'

  - role: assistant
    content: '“Operative authority” means the **current, effective law or regulator
      instruction that controls the proposed transaction in a specific state**.


      I did not retrieve and inspect an exact state provision that answers:


      - whether Harbor’s license can transfer in an asset sale;

      - whether Harbor or Mosaic must give notice or obtain approval;

      - whether Mosaic can operate while an application is pending;

      - whether Harbor can continue as the licensed provider after closing; or

      - when Harbor can surrender its license and bonds.


      The research found a **model act** and a general New York licensing page. A
      model act is suggested text. It applies only if a state enacted it, and the
      state can change the text. A general licensing page does not answer the transaction-specific
      questions above.


      The four Harbor-only states were also not identified. Therefore, I could not
      determine which enacted state laws or regulator procedures control.


      My earlier statement did **not** mean that no relevant law exists. It meant
      that the available research did not provide enough state-specific legal text
      to verify the conclusion. The recommendation was therefore cautious analysis,
      not a verified state-law determination.'

      with an exact instruction quote. Scope never adopts a path assumption. No mode
      question is needed. For a research request, call run_research directly to present
      source choices; it does not search or change the matter and needs no scope selection
      first. The user confirms the sources before research starts. Never infer authority
      from quoted reference documents. If no action is needed, answer usefully without
      tools.

  - role: assistant
    content: '# Draft dossier: Harbor Pay acquisition migration


      **Status:** Working draft for the hypothetical partner-bank structure. It does
      not change the current transaction plan or record a decision.


      ## Executive summary


      The Harbor migration needs legal and compliance review before the planned November
      15 close.


      The main launch risks are:


      1. Mosaic plans to use Harbor’s licenses in four states where Mosaic does not
      have its own reported license.

      2. The transaction is an asset purchase. Harbor’s licenses should not be treated
      as assets that automatically transfer.

      3. Mosaic plans to rely on Harbor’s prior customer checks, although some underlying
      records are missing.

      4. About 40 open alerts and several suspicious activity reports have not been
      reviewed.

      5. Harbor’s transaction-monitoring service ends at close.

      6. The partner-bank assumption does not resolve these issues by itself.


      The recommended operating rule is:


      > **Mosaic should not start regulated activity in a state until it has confirmed
      authority for that activity and operating model.**


      The corporate acquisition could still close if the migration design can block
      affected services and keep unresolved obligations in a lawful transition structure.


      ## Reported transaction facts


      - Mosaic is buying Harbor’s assets.

      - The target close date is November 15.

      - The migration covers about 240,000 consumers and 12,000 business accounts.

      - Users are expected to receive one Mosaic account, one balance, and access
      to Mosaic’s products on day one.

      - Mosaic does not plan to repeat Harbor’s customer identity checks.

      - Mosaic has pass/fail results but lacks underlying documents for some users
      onboarded before 2023.

      - Harbor’s monitoring contract ends at close.

      - Harbor has about 40 open alerts and several suspicious activity reports that
      remain unread.

      - Mosaic plans to use or assume Harbor’s licenses in four additional states.

      - Marketing proposes a $50 bonus for a customer’s first transfer.


      ## Hypothetical assumption


      For this dossier only, a partner bank holds customer funds.


      This assumption does not establish that the bank is the legal service provider.
      The result still depends on who contracts with customers, controls transfers,
      maintains balances, owes refunds, and performs compliance duties.


      ## Working recommendation


      Separate the acquisition closing from the launch of regulated services.


      Before Mosaic migrates customers in a state, require one of these outcomes:


      1. Mosaic’s existing license is confirmed to cover the full post-close activity.

      2. Mosaic obtains the required license or approval.

      3. The regulator confirms a lawful transition structure.

      4. Harbor remains the actual licensed provider under a supported run-off plan.

      5. The product blocks regulated activity until authority exists.


      Use a state launch hold for the four Harbor-only states unless the required
      authority is documented.


      ## Material issues


      ### State licensing


      The four Harbor-only states present the clearest launch risk. The affected states
      have not been identified, and no controlling state provision has been reviewed.


      The state analysis must cover more than rules labeled “license transfer.” It
      should examine asset-sale notices, buyer applications, changes in business plans,
      assignment of customer obligations, interim operation, bonds, and license surrender.


      **Support gap:** No current state-specific law or regulator instruction has
      been verified for the proposed transaction.


      ### Partner-bank structure


      Bank custody can affect the analysis, but custody alone does not give Mosaic
      permission to conduct regulated activity.


      The documents and operating model must establish:


      - who provides the service to customers;

      - who accepts transfer instructions;

      - who controls the bank account;

      - who records customer balances;

      - who owes repayment or refunds;

      - who bears failed-transfer losses;

      - whether the bank approved the migration; and

      - how compliance duties are allocated.


      A genuine bank-led program could change the licensing analysis. A bank account
      that only holds funds for Mosaic or Harbor might not.


      ### Customer identification and records


      Mosaic has not confirmed whether its compliance program permits reliance on
      Harbor’s prior identity checks. The missing pre-2023 records could prevent Mosaic
      from supporting that reliance or meeting its own record requirements.


      Compliance should determine which customers require new checks, additional records,
      or restricted access before migration.


      ### Open alerts and suspicious activity reports


      The unread alerts are a pre-close diligence priority. They could reveal unresolved
      suspicious activity, customer restrictions, reporting gaps, or weaknesses in
      Harbor’s monitoring program.


      The review should identify:


      - the subject customers and transactions;

      - the age and reason for each alert;

      - whether required action was completed;

      - whether any report requires follow-up;

      - whether affected accounts should migrate; and

      - whether the findings affect deal terms or closing conditions.


      ### Monitoring transition


      Repointing a transaction feed on day one does not show that monitoring will
      work correctly.


      Before migration, Mosaic should test data completeness, rule coverage, customer-history
      transfer, alert generation, case routing, sanctions controls, and escalation
      procedures. A tested fallback is needed if the feed or monitoring rules fail.


      ### Customer terms and communications


      The unified account design may change the customer’s service provider, contract,
      products, fees, privacy terms, and complaint contact.


      Legal should review contract assignment, required notices, customer consent,
      balance presentation, privacy disclosures, and the description of the partner
      bank’s role.


      ### Welcome bonus


      The $50 offer needs review after marketing defines the eligibility rules, transfer
      conditions, payment timing, exclusions, expiration terms, and required disclosures.


      The campaign should not launch until the team confirms that the advertised benefit
      matches the actual customer experience.


      ## Pre-close conditions


      1. Identify the four Harbor-only states.

      2. Complete a state-by-state licensing and filing matrix.

      3. Document the customer, funds, and legal-obligation flow.

      4. Confirm the partner bank’s role and obtain any required consent.

      5. Review the open alerts and suspicious activity report history.

      6. Decide how Mosaic will handle customers with missing identity records.

      7. Test the monitoring migration and fallback process.

      8. Confirm customer contract assignment and notice requirements.

      9. Place state and customer restrictions in the product where needed.

      10. Delay the bonus campaign until its terms and funds flow are final.


      ## Available fallback paths


      **State launch hold**


      Close the acquisition but block regulated functions in states where Mosaic lacks
      confirmed authority.


      **Harbor transition or run-off**


      Keep Harbor as the actual licensed provider for a limited period, if regulators,
      contracts, the bank, and operations support that structure.


      **Bank-led program**


      Make the partner bank the real service provider. This would require the contracts,
      customer disclosures, controls, and actual operations to match that model.


      ## Material unknowns


      - The identities of the four Harbor-only states.

      - The controlling requirements in each affected state.

      - Whether the bank or Mosaic contracts directly with users.

      - Who controls transfer instructions and settlement.

      - Who owes customers their balances and refunds.

      - Whether Mosaic’s compliance program permits reliance on Harbor’s checks.

      - What the unread alerts and report history show.

      - Whether the monitoring migration has been tested.

      - The final terms of the welcome bonus.


      ## Next step


      Identify the four Harbor-only states. Then prepare one integrated matrix covering
      licensing authority, required filings, interim operation, bank custody, outstanding
      customer obligations, and license surrender.


      ```problem-analysis

      acquisition closing and the regulated-service launch should be treated as separate
      milestones. Partner-bank custody does not by itself establish who provides the

      is buying Harbor’s assets rather than Harbor’s equity.","category":"relationship","status":"reported"},{"key":"day_one_migration","label":"Day-one

      custody","description":"For this hypothetical only, a partner bank holds customer

      transition","description":"Prior identity records are incomplete, open alerts
      remain unread, and Harbor’s monitoring service ends at close.","category":"constraint","status":"reported"}],"questions":[{"key":"state_authority","question":"What

  - role: assistant
    content: '# Draft dossier: Harbor Pay acquisition migration


      **Status:** Working draft. It uses the hypothetical assumption that a partner
      bank holds customer funds. It does not change the current transaction plan or
      record a decision.


      ## Executive summary


      The Harbor migration needs legal and compliance review before the planned November
      15 close.


      Mosaic should not:


      - assume that Harbor’s state licenses transfer in the asset purchase;

      - launch in the four Harbor-only states without confirmed authority;

      - rely on Harbor’s prior identity checks until compliance approves that approach;

      - migrate customers linked to unresolved alerts without review; or

      - depend on an untested day-one monitoring switch.


      A partner bank holding funds does not resolve these issues by itself. The key
      question is who provides the service, controls transfers, and owes the customer.


      **Working recommendation:** Separate the acquisition closing from the launch
      of regulated services. Block affected services where authority or compliance
      readiness is not confirmed.


      ## Reported facts


      - Mosaic is buying Harbor’s assets.

      - Closing is targeted for November 15.

      - About 240,000 consumers and 12,000 business accounts will move on day one.

      - Users will receive one account, one balance, and access to Mosaic’s products.

      - Mosaic plans no customer re-verification.

      - Pass/fail identity-check results exist, but some pre-2023 supporting documents
      are missing.

      - Harbor’s transaction-monitoring contract ends at close.

      - About 40 alerts and several suspicious activity reports remain unread.

      - Mosaic plans to use or assume Harbor’s licenses in four additional states.

      - Marketing proposes a $50 first-transfer bonus.


      ## Hypothetical assumption


      A partner bank holds customer funds.


      This does not establish that the bank is the legal service provider. That depends
      on the contracts and actual operations, including:


      - who contracts with users;

      - who accepts transfer instructions;

      - who controls settlement;

      - who records balances;

      - who owes refunds;

      - who handles complaints and losses; and

      - who performs customer checks, monitoring, alert review, and reporting.


      ## Main issues


      ### State licensing


      Harbor’s licenses should not be treated as assets that automatically move to
      Mosaic.


      The four Harbor-only states present the clearest launch risk. Mosaic should
      require its own authority, an approved transition, or another confirmed legal
      basis before offering regulated services there.


      The state review should cover asset-sale notices, buyer applications, changes
      in business plans, customer-obligation transfers, bonds, interim operations,
      and license surrender.


      **Support limit:** The four states have not been identified. No current state-specific
      rule or regulator instruction has been verified for this transaction.


      ### Partner-bank structure


      Bank custody can affect the analysis, but it is not automatically a licensing
      exemption.


      A genuine bank-led program could produce a different result if the bank is the
      actual provider and Mosaic performs only permitted support services. The contracts,
      customer terms, account control, and operations must all support that structure.


      ### Customer identification records


      Compliance has not confirmed whether Mosaic’s program permits reliance on Harbor’s
      prior checks.


      Before migration, compliance should determine:


      - whether reliance is permitted;

      - which records Mosaic must hold;

      - which customers need new checks;

      - whether missing records can be repaired; and

      - whether affected accounts need temporary restrictions.


      ### Alerts and suspicious activity reports


      The unread alerts and report history are a pre-close diligence priority.


      The review could identify unresolved suspicious activity, reporting gaps, restricted
      customers, or broader weaknesses in Harbor’s compliance program. Findings could
      affect customer migration, closing conditions, deal protections, or price.


      ### Monitoring transition


      Repointing Harbor’s transaction feed on day one does not prove that monitoring
      will work.


      Mosaic should test:


      - data completeness;

      - customer-history transfer;

      - monitoring-rule coverage;

      - alert generation;

      - case routing;

      - sanctions controls;

      - escalation procedures; and

      - the fallback process if the connection fails.


      ### Customer terms and communications


      The unified Mosaic account may change the service provider, contract, product
      set, fees, privacy terms, and complaint contact.


      Legal should review contract assignment, required notices, consent, balance
      presentation, privacy disclosures, and statements about the partner bank.


      ### Welcome bonus


      The $50 campaign needs review when its final terms are available.


      Marketing must define eligibility, qualifying transfers, payment timing, exclusions,
      expiration, and disclosures. The offer should not launch until the advertised
      benefit matches the actual product rules.


      ## Recommended pre-close conditions


      1. Identify the four Harbor-only states.

      2. Complete a state-by-state authority and filing matrix.

      3. Confirm the legal service provider after closing.

      4. Document the full funds and customer-obligation flow.

      5. Review the partner-bank agreements and required consents.

      6. Review the open alerts and suspicious activity report history.

      7. Decide how to handle customers with missing identity records.

      8. Test the monitoring transition and fallback.

      9. confirm customer contract and notice requirements.

      10. Add state and customer restrictions where needed.


      ## Fallback paths


      **State launch hold:** Close the acquisition, but block regulated services where
      Mosaic lacks confirmed authority.


      **Harbor transition:** Keep Harbor as the actual licensed provider temporarily,
      where the contracts, regulators, bank, and operations permit it.


      **Bank-led program:** Make the partner bank the actual provider. This would
      require material changes to contracts, controls, customer disclosures, and operations.


      ## Working conclusion


      The acquisition can potentially close before every customer is migrated. Mosaic
      should treat legal authority and compliance readiness as launch conditions.


      The safest working rule is:


      > **No confirmed authority and no tested compliance coverage means no day-one
      regulated service for the affected customer.**


      The next step is to identify the four Harbor-only states and prepare one combined
      licensing, funds-flow, and compliance-readiness matrix.'

        Separate the acquisition closing from the launch of regulated services.


        Before Mosaic migrates customers in a state, require one of these outcomes:


        1. Mosaic’s existing license is confirmed to cover the full post-close activity.

        2. Mosaic obtains the required license or approval.

        3. The regulator confirms a lawful transition structure.

        4. Harbor remains the actual licensed provider under a supported run-off plan.

        5. The product blocks regulated activity until authority exists.


        Use a state launch hold for the four Harbor-only states unless the required
        authority is documented.


        ## Material issues


        ### State licensing


        The four Harbor-only states present the clearest launch risk. The affected
        states have not been identified, and no controlling state provision has been
        reviewed.


        The state analysis must cover more than rules labeled “license transfer.”
        It should examine asset-sale notices, buyer applications, changes in business
        plans, assignment of customer obligations, interim operation, bonds, and license
        surrender.


        **Support gap:** No current state-specific law or regulator instruction has
        been verified for the proposed transaction.


        ### Partner-bank structure


        Bank custody can affect the analysis, but custody alone does not give Mosaic
        permission to conduct regulated activity.


        The documents and operating model must establish:


        - who provides the service to customers;

        - who accepts transfer instructions;

        - who controls the bank account;

        - who records customer balances;

        - who owes repayment or refunds;

        - who bears failed-transfer losses;

        - whether the bank approved the migration; and

        - how compliance duties are allocated.


        A genuine bank-led program could change the licensing analysis. A bank account
        that only holds funds for Mosaic or Harbor might not.


        ### Customer identification and records


        Mosaic has not confirmed whether its compliance program permits reliance on
        Harbor’s prior identity checks. The missing pre-2023 records could prevent
        Mosaic from supporting that reliance or meeting its own record requirements.


        Compliance should determine which customers require new checks, additional
        records, or restricted access before migration.


        ### Open alerts and suspicious activity reports


        The unread alerts are a pre-close diligence priority. They could reveal unresolved
        suspicious activity, customer restrictions, reporting gaps, or weaknesses
        in Harbor’s monitoring program.


        The review should identify:


        - the subject customers and transactions;

        - the age and reason for each alert;

        - whether required action was completed;

        - whether any report requires follow-up;

        - whether affected accounts should migrate; and

        - whether the findings affect deal terms or closing conditions.


        ### Monitoring transition


        Repointing a transaction feed on day one does not show that monitoring will
        work correctly.


        Before migration, Mosaic should test data completeness, rule coverage, customer-history
        transfer, alert generation, case routing, sanctions controls, and escalation
        procedures. A tested fallback is needed if the feed or monitoring rules fail.


        ### Customer terms and communications


        The unified account design may change the customer’s service provider, contract,
        products, fees, privacy terms, and complaint contact.


        Legal should review contract assignment, required notices, customer consent,
        balance presentation, privacy disclosures, and the description of the partner
        bank’s role.


        ### Welcome bonus


        The $50 offer needs review after marketing defines the eligibility rules,
        transfer conditions, payment timing, exclusions, expiration terms, and required
        disclosures.


        The campaign should not launch until the team confirms that the advertised
        benefit matches the actual customer experience.


        ## Pre-close conditions


        1. Identify the four Harbor-only states.

        2. Complete a state-by-state licensing and filing matrix.

        3. Document the customer, funds, and legal-obligation flow.

        4. Confirm the partner bank’s role and obtain any required consent.

        5. Review the open alerts and suspicious activity report history.

        6. Decide how Mosaic will handle customers with missing identity records.

        7. Test the monitoring migration and fallback process.

        8. Confirm customer contract assignment and notice requirements.

        9. Place state and customer restrictions in the product where needed.

        10. Delay the bonus campaign until its terms and funds flow are final.


        ## Available fallback paths


        **State launch hold**


        Close the acquisition but block regulated functions in states where Mosaic
        lacks confirmed authority.


        **Harbor transition or run-off**


        Keep Harbor as the actual licensed provider for a limited period, if regulators,
        contracts, the bank, and operations support that structure.


        **Bank-led program**


        Make the partner bank the real service provider. This would require the contracts,
        customer disclosures, controls, and actual operations to match that model.


        ## Material unknowns


        - The identities of the four Harbor-only states.

        - The controlling requirements in each affected state.

        - Whether the bank or Mosaic contracts directly with users.

        - Who controls transfer instructions and settlement.

        - Who owes customers their balances and refunds.

        - Whether Mosaic’s compliance program permits reliance on Harbor’s checks.

        - What the unread alerts and report history show.

        - Whether the monitoring migration has been tested.

        - The final terms of the welcome bonus.


        ## Next step


        Identify the four Harbor-only states. Then prepare one integrated matrix covering
        licensing authority, required filings, interim operation, bank custody, outstanding
        customer obligations, and license surrender.

    result_ref:
      content: '# Draft dossier: Harbor Pay acquisition migration


        **Status:** Working draft. It uses the hypothetical assumption that a partner
        bank holds customer funds. It does not change the current transaction plan
        or record a decision.


        ## Executive summary


        The Harbor migration needs legal and compliance review before the planned
        November 15 close.


        Mosaic should not:


        - assume that Harbor’s state licenses transfer in the asset purchase;

        - launch in the four Harbor-only states without confirmed authority;

        - rely on Harbor’s prior identity checks until compliance approves that approach;

        - migrate customers linked to unresolved alerts without review; or

        - depend on an untested day-one monitoring switch.


        A partner bank holding funds does not resolve these issues by itself. The
        key question is who provides the service, controls transfers, and owes the
        customer.


        **Working recommendation:** Separate the acquisition closing from the launch
        of regulated services. Block affected services where authority or compliance
        readiness is not confirmed.


        ## Reported facts


        - Mosaic is buying Harbor’s assets.

        - Closing is targeted for November 15.

        - About 240,000 consumers and 12,000 business accounts will move on day one.

        - Users will receive one account, one balance, and access to Mosaic’s products.

        - Mosaic plans no customer re-verification.

        - Pass/fail identity-check results exist, but some pre-2023 supporting documents
        are missing.

        - Harbor’s transaction-monitoring contract ends at close.

        - About 40 alerts and several suspicious activity reports remain unread.

        - Mosaic plans to use or assume Harbor’s licenses in four additional states.

        - Marketing proposes a $50 first-transfer bonus.


        ## Hypothetical assumption


        A partner bank holds customer funds.


        This does not establish that the bank is the legal service provider. That
        depends on the contracts and actual operations, including:


        - who contracts with users;

        - who accepts transfer instructions;

        - who controls settlement;

        - who records balances;

        - who owes refunds;

        - who handles complaints and losses; and

        - who performs customer checks, monitoring, alert review, and reporting.


        ## Main issues


        ### State licensing


        Harbor’s licenses should not be treated as assets that automatically move
        to Mosaic.


        The four Harbor-only states present the clearest launch risk. Mosaic should
        require its own authority, an approved transition, or another confirmed legal
        basis before offering regulated services there.


        The state review should cover asset-sale notices, buyer applications, changes
        in business plans, customer-obligation transfers, bonds, interim operations,
        and license surrender.


        **Support limit:** The four states have not been identified. No current state-specific
        rule or regulator instruction has been verified for this transaction.


        ### Partner-bank structure


        Bank custody can affect the analysis, but it is not automatically a licensing
        exemption.


        A genuine bank-led program could produce a different result if the bank is
        the actual provider and Mosaic performs only permitted support services. The
        contracts, customer terms, account control, and operations must all support
        that structure.


        ### Customer identification records


        Compliance has not confirmed whether Mosaic’s program permits reliance on
        Harbor’s prior checks.


        Before migration, compliance should determine:


        - whether reliance is permitted;

        - which records Mosaic must hold;

        - which customers need new checks;

        - whether missing records can be repaired; and

        - whether affected accounts need temporary restrictions.


        ### Alerts and suspicious activity reports


        The unread alerts and report history are a pre-close diligence priority.


        The review could identify unresolved suspicious activity, reporting gaps,
        restricted customers, or broader weaknesses in Harbor’s compliance program.
        Findings could affect customer migration, closing conditions, deal protections,
        or price.


        ### Monitoring transition


        Repointing Harbor’s transaction feed on day one does not prove that monitoring
        will work.


        Mosaic should test:


        - data completeness;

        - customer-history transfer;

        - monitoring-rule coverage;

        - alert generation;

        - case routing;

        - sanctions controls;

        - escalation procedures; and

        - the fallback process if the connection fails.


        ### Customer terms and communications


        The unified Mosaic account may change the service provider, contract, product
        set, fees, privacy terms, and complaint contact.


        Legal should review contract assignment, required notices, consent, balance
        presentation, privacy disclosures, and statements about the partner bank.


        ### Welcome bonus


        The $50 campaign needs review when its final terms are available.


        Marketing must define eligibility, qualifying transfers, payment timing, exclusions,
        expiration, and disclosures. The offer should not launch until the advertised
        benefit matches the actual product rules.


        ## Recommended pre-close conditions


        1. Identify the four Harbor-only states.

        2. Complete a state-by-state authority and filing matrix.

        3. Confirm the legal service provider after closing.

        4. Document the full funds and customer-obligation flow.

        5. Review the partner-bank agreements and required consents.

        6. Review the open alerts and suspicious activity report history.

        7. Decide how to handle customers with missing identity records.

        8. Test the monitoring transition and fallback.

        9. confirm customer contract and notice requirements.

        10. Add state and customer restrictions where needed.


        ## Fallback paths


        **State launch hold:** Close the acquisition, but block regulated services
        where Mosaic lacks confirmed authority.


        **Harbor transition:** Keep Harbor as the actual licensed provider temporarily,
        where the contracts, regulators, bank, and operations permit it.


        **Bank-led program:** Make the partner bank the actual provider. This would
        require material changes to contracts, controls, customer disclosures, and
        operations.


        ## Working conclusion


        The acquisition can potentially close before every customer is migrated. Mosaic
        should treat legal authority and compliance readiness as launch conditions.


        The safest working rule is:


        > **No confirmed authority and no tested compliance coverage means no day-one
        regulated service for the affected customer.**


        The next step is to identify the four Harbor-only states and prepare one combined
        licensing, funds-flow, and compliance-readiness matrix.'

  ### Alerts and suspicious activity reports


  The unread alerts and report history are a pre-close diligence priority.


  The review could identify unresolved suspicious activity, reporting gaps, restricted
  customers, or broader weaknesses in Harbor’s compliance program. Findings could
  affect customer migration, closing conditions, deal protections, or price.


  ### Monitoring transition


  Repointing Harbor’s transaction feed on day one does not prove that monitoring will
  work.


  Mosaic should test:


  - data completeness;

  - customer-history transfer;

  - monitoring-rule coverage;

  - alert generation;

  - case routing;

  - sanctions controls;

  - escalation procedures; and

  - the fallback process if the connection fails.


  ### Customer terms and communications


  The unified Mosaic account may change the service provider, contract, product set,
  fees, privacy terms, and complaint contact.


  Legal should review contract assignment, required notices, consent, balance presentation,
  privacy disclosures, and statements about the partner bank.


  ### Welcome bonus


  The $50 campaign needs review when its final terms are available.


  Marketing must define eligibility, qualifying transfers, payment timing, exclusions,
  expiration, and disclosures. The offer should not launch until the advertised benefit
  matches the actual product rules.


  ## Recommended pre-close conditions


  1. Identify the four Harbor-only states.

  2. Complete a state-by-state authority and filing matrix.

  3. Confirm the legal service provider after closing.

  4. Document the full funds and customer-obligation flow.

  5. Review the partner-bank agreements and required consents.

  6. Review the open alerts and suspicious activity report history.

  7. Decide how to handle customers with missing identity records.

  8. Test the monitoring transition and fallback.

  9. confirm customer contract and notice requirements.

  10. Add state and customer restrictions where needed.


  ## Fallback paths


  **State launch hold:** Close the acquisition, but block regulated services where
  Mosaic lacks confirmed authority.


  **Harbor transition:** Keep Harbor as the actual licensed provider temporarily,
  where the contracts, regulators, bank, and operations permit it.


  **Bank-led program:** Make the partner bank the actual provider. This would require
  material changes to contracts, controls, customer disclosures, and operations.


  ## Working conclusion


  The acquisition can potentially close before every customer is migrated. Mosaic
  should treat legal authority and compliance readiness as launch conditions.


  The safest working rule is:


  > **No confirmed authority and no tested compliance coverage means no day-one regulated
  service for the affected customer.**


  The next step is to identify the four Harbor-only states and prepare one combined
  licensing, funds-flow, and compliance-readiness matrix.'
